[^347]: Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera: conclusion of the Fourth Matins Resurrection Gospel from Luke (Luke 24:9–12); followed by Exapostilarion 4 (*Добродѣтельми блиставше...* / Greek: *Ταῖς ἀρεтаῖς ἀστрάψанτες*); Theotokion (*В утробу Твою, Пречистая...*); and the opening of Evangelical Sticheron 4 in Tone 4 (*Утро бѣ глубоко...* / Greek: *Ὄρθρος ἦν βαθύς*). Note Fiol's corrupted typesetting in the sticheron: *Оутренѣ бѣ бракѡ* (for *Утро бѣ глубоко*).

[^348]: Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera: conclusion of Evangelical Sticheron 4 in Tone 4; followed by the Fifth Matins Resurrection Gospel from Luke (Luke 24:12–25), detailing the journey of Cleopas and his companion to Emmaus and their encounter with the risen Christ.

[^349]: Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera: conclusion of the Fifth Matins Resurrection Gospel from Luke (Luke 24:23–35), recounting the journey of Cleopas and Luke to Emmaus, Christ expounding the Scriptures, His recognition in the breaking of bread, and the report of the apostles in Jerusalem; followed by Exapostilarion 5 (*Живот и путь Христос...* / Greek: *Ἡ ζωὴ καὶ ἡ ὁδὸς Χριστὸς...*).

[^350]: Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera: conclusion of Exapostilarion 5; followed by its Theotokion (*Творяй Господь небеса всяческим...* / Greek: *Ὁ ποιητὴς τῶν ποιημάτων...*); Evangelical Sticheron 5 in Tone 5 (*О премудрых судеб Твоих, Христе!...* / Greek: *Ὢ τῶν σοφῶν σου κριμάτων, Χριστέ...*); and the opening of the Sixth Matins Resurrection Gospel from Luke (Luke 24:36–40).

[^351]: Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera: conclusion of the Sixth Matins Resurrection Gospel from Luke (Luke 24:41–53), describing Christ eating the broiled fish and honeycomb before the disciples, expounding the Scriptures, blessing them at Bethany, and ascending into heaven; followed by Exapostilarion 6 (*Показуя яко Человѣк еси, Спасе...* / Greek: *Δεικνύων ὅτι ἄνθρωπος, Σῶтер...*).

[^352]: Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera: conclusion of Exapostilarion 6; followed by its Theotokion (*Своего видящи от мертвых воставша...* / Greek: *Ὡς ἴδεν ἐκ νекрῶν ἀναστάντα...*); Evangelical Sticheron 6 in Tone 6 (*Истинно миръ Ты еси, Христе...* / Greek: *Ἡ ὄντως εἰρήνη σὺ Χριστέ...*); and the opening of the Seventh Matins Resurrection Gospel from John (John 20:1–5).

[^353]: Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera: conclusion of the Seventh Matins Resurrection Gospel from John (John 20:5–10); followed by Exapostilarion 7 (*Яко взяша Господа, Марии рекшей...* / Greek: *Ὅτι ἦран τὸν Κύριον...*); its Theotokion (*По неизреченному человеколюбию...*), noting Fiol's corrupted typesetting in the incipit (*Поютвожесъчнеле*); and Evangelical Sticheron 7 in Tone 7 (*Се тма и утро, и что при гробе, Марие, стоиши...* / Greek: *Ἰδοὺ σκοτία καὶ πρωΐ...*), with Fiol's variant *Сетлаи рано* for *Се тма и рано*.

[^354]: Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera: Eighth Matins Resurrection Gospel from John (John 20:11–18), recounting Christ's appearance to Mary Magdalene at the sepulchre (*Noli me tangere*); followed by Exapostilarion 8 (*Два ангела видѣвши внутрь гроба...* / Greek: *Δύο Ἀγγέλους βлέψασα...*); and the opening incipit of its Theotokion, noting Fiol's typesetting error *бьспѣдрь* for *нѣдръ* (*Отческих нѣдр не отступив...*).

[^355]: Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera: conclusion of the Theotokion of Exapostilarion 8; followed by Evangelical Sticheron 8 in Tone 8 (*Мариины слезы не всуе проливаются...* / Greek: *Τὰ τῆς Μαρίας δάκρυα...*); and the opening of the Ninth Matins Resurrection Gospel from John (John 20:19–25), recounting Christ's appearance to the disciples through closed doors and the absence of Thomas.

[^356]: Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera: conclusion of the Ninth Matins Resurrection Gospel from John (John 20:25–31), recording the confession of Thomas (*My Lord and my God*) and the Johannine purpose statement; followed by Exapostilarion 9 (*Затвореным, Владыко, дверем...* / Greek: *Συγκεκλεισμένων Δέσποта...*); its Theotokion (*Богородительнице чистая Дѣво, скорбящым радосте...*); and the opening of Evangelical Sticheron 9 in Tone 5 (*Яко в послѣдняя времена...* / Greek: *Ὡς ἐπ' ἐσχάτων τῶν χρόνων...*).

[^357]: Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera: conclusion of Evangelical Sticheron 9 in Tone 5; followed by the opening of the Tenth Matins Resurrection Gospel from John (John 21:1–8), recounting the appearance of the risen Christ by the Sea of Tiberias and the miraculous catch of fish.

[^358]: Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera: conclusion of the Tenth Matins Resurrection Gospel from John (John 21:8–14), recording the 153 great fishes, the meal of bread and fish upon the coals, and Christ revealing Himself for the third time; followed by Exapostilarion 10 (*Тивериадское море с дѣтьми Зеведеовыми...* / Greek: *Τιβεριάδος θάλασσα...*); its Theotokion (*Покланяемся, Пречистая, Твоему честному образу...*); and the rubric for Evangelical Sticheron 10 in Tone 6 (*Стихира евангельская 10, глас 6*).

[^359]: Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera: conclusion of Evangelical Sticheron 10 in Tone 6 (*По еже во ад сошествии...* / Greek: *Μετὰ τὴν εἰς ᾅδου κάθοδον...*), noting Fiol's defective typesetting *и лованнїа ло* for *и лова нигдеже*; followed by the opening of the Eleventh Matins Resurrection Gospel from John (John 21:15–19), recounting Christ's threefold question to Peter (*Simon, son of Jonas, lovest thou Me?*), the pastoral commission (*Feed My sheep*), and the prophecy of Peter's martyrdom.

[^360]: Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera: conclusion of the Eleventh Matins Resurrection Gospel from John (John 21:20–25), recording Peter's question concerning John, Christ's response, and the Johannine conclusion; followed by Exapostilarion 11 (*По божественнѣм Ти востании...* / Greek: *Μετὰ τὴν θείαν Ἔγερσιν...*); its Theotokion (*Радуйся, воспоем ти, Пречистая Богородице...*); and the rubric for Evangelical Sticheron 11 in Tone 8 (*Стихира евангельская 11, глас 8*).

[^361]: Exapostilaria and Evangelical Stichera conclusion and beginning of the Weekday Lenten Hymns to the Trinity (Triadica / *Троичны*): text of Evangelical Sticheron 11 in Tone 8 (*Являя Себе учеником, Спасе...* / Greek: *Φανερῶν ἑαυτὸν...*); followed by the rubrics and Scriptural verses from Isaiah 26 for the Matins Alleluia during Fasts; the Troparia to the Trinity in Tone 1 with their sessional verse; and the opening of the Troparia to the Trinity in Tone 2 (*Несозданное Естество, всяческих Содѣтелю...*).

[^362]: Weekday Lenten Hymns to the Trinity: conclusion of Tone 2 Troparia to the Trinity with its Photogogic (*Свет Твой трисолнечный посли, Господи...*); complete Troparia to the Trinity in Tone 3 with Photogogic (*Посли свет Твой, Христе Боже...*); and Tone 4 Troparia to the Trinity (*Безначальнаго Ти Отца...*).

[^363]: Weekday Lenten Hymns to the Trinity: conclusion of Tone 4 Troparia to the Trinity and Photogogic; complete Troparia to the Trinity in Tone 5 with Photogogic (*Светодавче Господи, посли свет Твой...*), noting Fiol's typesetting error *в ложе спѣдвчи влⷣцьем* for *в ложеснах дѣвичих, Владыко*; and the opening of Tone 6 Troparia to the Trinity.

[^364]: Weekday Lenten Hymns to the Trinity: conclusion of Tone 6 Troparia to the Trinity and Photogogic; complete Troparia to the Trinity in Tone 7 with Photogogic (*Предстательством безплотных, свет низпосли душам нашим...*); and Tone 8 Troparia to the Trinity (*На небеси оружничествующе...*).

[^365]: Conclusion of the Weekday Lenten Hymns to the Trinity (Tone 8 Photogogic *Свет сый, Христе, просвети мя...*); followed by the heading and opening of the ascetic homily attributed to Saint Ephrem the Syrian (*Слово святаго Ефрема: Отца, Господи, благослови, отче...*), exhorting Christians to compunction in remembrance of the Dread Judgment, and opening the series of Woes against transgressors.

[^366]: Discourse of Saint Ephrem: continuation of the Woes, denouncing fraternal hatred, desecration of Sunday, envy, slander, theft, beastly fornication, usury (*лихвоу по насилию рѣꙁъ емлють*), incest and defilement of nuns, bribery, extortion, unrepentant grudge-bearing, irreverent chatter and uncrossed bows in church, self-righteousness, and murder.

[^367]: Discourse of Saint Ephrem: continuation of the Woes, denouncing unjust enslavement, perjury, cruelty to servants, Sunday absenteeism, mockery of sacred ministers, wrath, profaning oaths by holy hierarchs, clerical breaking of the Eucharistic fast before the sixth hour, hypocritical judgment of others, hard-heartedness toward the poor and strangers, pastoral neglect of spiritual children, and drunkenness.

[^368]: Discourse of Saint Ephrem: concluding Woe against neglecting the care of the soul; followed by the Saint's personal lamentation of compunction (*О лютѣ мнѣ, грѣшнику и небогу...*), mourning his spiritual blindness, false humility coupled with arrogance, lack of fasting and prayer, failure to emulate the virtuous, and inclination toward the spiritually lax.

[^369]: Conclusion of the homily on repentance and the Dread Judgment attributed to Saint Ephrem the Syrian (opening on leaf p327): the Saint continues his personal lamentation of compunction, bewailing the soul's surrender to bodily lusts, unfaithfulness, and spiritual negligence, and invoking the Beatitudes from the Sermon on the Mount (Matthew 5:3–12 / Luke 6:20–23) as the divine standard of salvation.

[^370]: Conclusion of the homily on the Judgment, contrasting the eternal punishment of the slothful with the eternal blessedness of the righteous and concluding with a Trinitarian doxology; followed by the heading and opening of the pedagogical discourse on childrearing, *Discourse on How It Behoves Parents to Instruct Their Children* (*Слово како подобаеть родителемъ наказнити чада своꙗ*), drawn from the Wisdom of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus 30:1–13; 7:24–25; Proverbs 23:13–14) and patristic florilegia (the *Izmaragd* and *Zlatoust* tradition).

[^371]: Conclusion of the pedagogical discourse on childrearing from Sirach 30, warning parents against indulging children and urging timely discipline; followed by the heading and opening of the ascetic discourse attributed to Saint John Chrysostom (*Слово святаго Іоанна Златоустаго: Господи, благослови, отче, прочести*), warning against complacency, worldly merrymaking, and false arguments that God created food and drink merely for self-indulgence.

[^372]: Discourse of Saint John Chrysostom: the preacher invokes the austere fasting of the Prophet Elijah on Mount Horeb (3 Kings / 1 Kings 19:8) and the ascetic detachment of the Prophet Daniel in Babylon (Daniel 1:8–16; 6:16–22), who spurned royal riches, precious stones, and morse ivory / walrus tusks (*рыбе зоубны*, a characteristic medieval East Slavic trade luxury) and was preserved unharmed in the lions' den; followed by an urgent rebuke against human pride, gluttony, and spiritual blindness.

[^373]: Discourse of Saint John Chrysostom: contrasting present temporal deprivation with future eternal reward (paraphrasing the Gospel Beatitudes and the parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus, Luke 16:25); exhorting the faithful to guard the tongue, eyes, and ears; lamenting the soul trapped within a pampered body; and appealing to Christ the Physician, Who raised Lazarus four days dead (John 11:39–44), urging repentance before the final accounting.

[^374]: Conclusion of the discourse of Saint John Chrysostom: citing the divine challenge from the Book of Job (Job 38:4); comparing fleeting youth to the morning dew; warning of the sudden coming of death like a thief in the night (Matthew 24:43; 1 Thessalonians 5:2); providing a model prayer to the Lord Jesus Christ and the Most Holy Theotokos; and concluding with the solemn Trinitarian doxology.

[^375]: Quire register (registrum chartarum / collation table) of the 1491 Krakow Octoechos, entitled in 15th-century Ruthenian / Early Modern East Slavic: *То е́сть личба до то́й книгы, яко и мають тетради быти...* ("This is the register for this book, how the quires ought to be..."), listing in five columns the Cyrillic numerical signatures of all 84 gatherings (quires) constituting the complete volume (quires 1 [а҃] through 84 [п҃д], equivalent to 170 folios / 340 physical leaves), serving as an explicit guide for the bookbinder.

[^376]: Historic printer's colophon of the 1491 Krakow Octoechos (*Blatt 169b*): beneath the woodcut coat of arms of the City of Cracow (herb miasta Krakowa) flanked by banderoles bearing the initials `S. V.` (Schweipolt Veyl / Fiol) and the date `1491` and `FIOŁ`, the six-line printed Church Slavonic text records the completion of the printing of this first Cyrillic incunabulum in Cracow under the reign of King Casimir IV Jagiellon by the burgher Schweipolt Fiol, a Franconian German (*из нѣмець немецького родоу, франкъ*), in the year 1491 from the Nativity of Christ; followed by a 16th/17th-century humanist Latin manuscript inscription recording the same historical data.

[^377]: Authentic blank verso leaf (fol. 169v / p339) of handmade laid rag paper, showing the faint strike-through contour of the woodcut printer's device from the recto, and stamped with the 20th-century circular archival stamp of the All-Ukrainian National Library (Всенародна бібліотека України / ВБУ, later the National Library of Ukraine named after V. I. Vernadsky) with manuscript inventory shelfmark `10423-47`.

[^378]: Photographic archival target slip (*ЭТОГО ЛИСТА В КНИГЕ НЕТ* — "This leaf is not in the book") inserted by the digitization / microfilming laboratory, indicating that the final physical leaf (blank endleaf / flyleaf, fol. 170v / p340) was wanting in this preserved exemplar of the 1491 Fiol Octoechos.

[^379]: Photographic archival target slip (*ЭТОГО ЛИСТА В КНИГЕ НЕТ* — "This leaf is not in the book") inserted by the microfilming laboratory, indicating that the original blank folio 171r (Blatt 171a) was wanting in the exemplar collated during microfilming. As documented in the 1866 Breslau collation certificate on leaf p345, folios 170, 171, and 172 were originally blank quires issued at the conclusion of the 1491 Krakow edition.

[^380]: Photographic archival target slip (*ЭТОГО ЛИСТА В КНИГЕ НЕТ* — "This leaf is not in the book") inserted by the microfilming laboratory, recording the absence of the blank verso folio 171v (Blatt 171b) in the filmed codex.

[^381]: Photographic archival target slip (*ЭТОГО ЛИСТА В КНИГЕ НЕТ* — "This leaf is not in the book") inserted by the microfilming laboratory, recording the absence of the recto of the final blank folio 172r (Blatt 172a).

[^382]: Photographic archival target slip (*ЭТОГО ЛИСТА В КНИГЕ НЕТ* — "This leaf is not in the book") inserted by the microfilming laboratory, recording the absence of the final blank verso folio 172v (Blatt 172b), concluding the physical text block of the incunabulum prior to the rear flyleaf and binding.

[^383]: Nineteenth-century German scholarly collation certificate (*Collationierungs-Attest*) mounted upon the rear flyleaf (leaf p345) from the Breslau Stadtbibliothek (now Biblioteka Uniwersytecka we Wrocławiu), dated 1866 (specifically recorded in library collation logs as 15 November 1866) and initialed *C.F.* (or *O.F.*). The note confirms that the complete exemplar of Schweipolt Fiol's 1491 Krakow *Octoechos* comprises 172 printed and blank leaves (corresponding to 84 quires as listed in the quire register on fol. 169r / leaf p337), of which fol. 1v (*Blatt 1b*) and fol. 169v (*Blatt 169b*) bear full-page woodcuts, while folios 170, 171, and 172 (*Blaetter 170. 171. u. 172*) conclude the codex as unprinted blank leaves (*sind leer*).
